Convexity in the first case follows either from Hölder’s inequality applied after a spatial translation, or by differentiating the logarithm: its second derivative is the tilted mean of the old second derivative plus $c$ times the tilted variance of the old first derivative. The first derivative is a tilted mean of the old first derivative. The assertions in the case $c=0$ follow directly from convolution.

<a id="eq:control-representation"></a>
<a id="eq:value-stability"></a>
For these step solutions, Itô’s formula and completion of the square give the control representation $$\Phi(t,x)=\sup_{|a|\leq1}\mathbb E\left[
 \psi\left(x+B_1-B_t+\int_t^1\gamma(s)a_s\,\mathrm ds\right)
 -\frac12\int_t^1\gamma(s)a_s^2\,\mathrm ds\right].
 \label{eq:control-representation}$$ The supremum is over progressively measurable controls. Indeed, the drift of $\Phi(s,Y_s)-\frac12\int_t^s\gamma a^2$ is $-\gamma(a-u_\gamma)^2/2$ when $\mathrm dY_s=\gamma(s)a_s\,\mathrm ds+\mathrm dB_s$. Equality is attained by $a_s=u_\gamma(s,Y_s)$; for the smooth finite-step problem the feedback equation has a unique strong solution. Consequently two such solutions obey $$\|\Phi_{\gamma,\psi}-\Phi_{\widetilde\gamma,\widetilde\psi}
       \|_\infty
 \leq\|\psi-\widetilde\psi\|_\infty
             +\frac32\|\gamma-\widetilde\gamma\|_1.
 \label{eq:value-stability}$$ Here the supremum is over the whole time-space domain, even though the individual functions grow linearly. The bound follows by using the same controls on both sides of <a href="#eq:control-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:control-representation">[eq:control-representation]</a>.

Nonnegative nondecreasing finite step functions are dense in $\mathcal U$ for the $L^1$ norm. For example, truncate at a time increasing to one, approximate the resulting bounded monotone function by monotone step functions, and then let the truncation time tend to one. Since $0\leq M^{-1}\log(2\cosh(Mx))-|x|\leq M^{-1}\log2$, <a href="#eq:value-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:value-stability">[eq:value-stability]</a> defines a unique uniform limit, with control representation <a href="#eq:control-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:control-representation">[eq:control-representation]</a> and terminal datum $|x|$. This agrees with the Parisi solution in the model definition. All the asserted shape properties pass to the limit. The zero control and Jensen’s inequality give the lower bound in <a href="#eq:terminal-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:terminal-uniform">[eq:terminal-uniform]</a>. For the upper bound use the triangle inequality and $|a|-a^2/2\leq1/2$ in the control representation.

<a id="eq:local-coefficient-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:burgers-mild"></a>
<a id="eq:fractional-bound"></a>
We give the smoothing estimates explicitly. This also establishes the uniformity needed below. On any strip $[0,b]$ with $b<1$, monotonicity gives $$\gamma(t)\leq\frac{\|\gamma\|_1}{1-b},\qquad 0\leq t\leq b.
 \label{eq:local-coefficient-bound}$$ Thus all coefficients in an $L^1$-bounded family have a common bound $\Gamma$ there. Work first with the smooth-terminal step solutions and put $U=\Phi_x$. Differentiation of the mild equation gives $$U(t)=P_{b-t}U(b)
       +\int_t^b\gamma(s)P_{s-t}[U(s)U_x(s)]\,\mathrm ds.
 \label{eq:burgers-mild}$$ We always have $\|U\|_\infty\leq1$, independently of its terminal smoothing. The heat-kernel estimate $\|\partial_xP_h f\|_\infty\leq C h^{-1/2}\|f\|_\infty$ implies $$\|U_x(t)\|_\infty
 \leq \frac{C}{\sqrt{b-t}}
       +C\Gamma\int_t^b
                  \frac{\|U_x(s)\|_\infty}{\sqrt{s-t}}\,\mathrm ds.
 \label{eq:fractional-bound}$$ The constants do not involve derivatives of the terminal datum.

For completeness, the integral inequality in <a href="#eq:fractional-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fractional-bound">[eq:fractional-bound]</a> gives a uniform bound away from $b$. The convolution of $h^{-1/2}$ with itself equals $\pi$. Substituting the inequality once into its integral therefore reduces it to an ordinary backward Gronwall inequality, with forcing bounded by a constant times $1+(b-t)^{-1/2}$. That forcing is integrable. The resulting estimate is uniform on every smaller strip.

The same argument bounds all higher derivatives. Suppose that derivatives of $U$ through order $m-1$ are already bounded on a slightly larger strip. Differentiate <a href="#eq:burgers-mild" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:burgers-mild">[eq:burgers-mild]</a> $m$ times, placing one derivative on the heat kernel and the other $m-1$ on $UU_x$. Its highest-order term is $U\partial_x^mU$; every remaining product involves derivatives of orders at most $m-1$. The initial term is bounded by $C(b-t)^{-1/2}\|\partial_x^{m-1}U(b)\|_\infty$. Thus the same integral inequality applies to $\|\partial_x^mU(t)\|_\infty$, with an additional bounded forcing. Choose successively smaller strip endpoints in this induction. For every fixed $m$ this proves the asserted bound, uniformly for the approximants.

<a id="eq:interpolation"></a>
Uniform convergence of the functions now gives convergence of all their spatial derivatives without a separate singular-kernel comparison. If a bounded $C^2$ function $F$ on $\mathbb R$ has bounded second derivative, Taylor’s formula gives, for every $h>0$, $$\|F'\|_\infty\leq
 \frac{2\|F\|_\infty}{h}
       +\frac h2\|F''\|_\infty.
 \label{eq:interpolation}$$ Apply this to a difference of two approximating solutions, uniformly on a fixed smaller strip. The uniform second-derivative bound and uniform convergence imply convergence of the first derivatives. Apply <a href="#eq:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interpolation">[eq:interpolation]</a> successively to the differences of the first, second, and later derivatives; the next two derivative bounds are available at every stage. This proves uniform convergence of every positive spatial derivative. Their limits are the spatial derivatives of the limiting function, by the fundamental theorem of calculus. Since the approximating derivatives are jointly continuous, so are their uniform limits.

The argument applies also to an arbitrary $L^1$-convergent sequence in $\mathcal U$: first obtain these bounds for each member by step approximation, then use <a href="#eq:value-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:value-stability">[eq:value-stability]</a>, the uniform local coefficient bound <a href="#eq:local-coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-coefficient-bound">[eq:local-coefficient-bound]</a>, and <a href="#eq:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interpolation">[eq:interpolation]</a>. Finally pass to the limit in the integral form of the differentiated equation on each compact strip. All its spatial factors converge uniformly, and its coefficient converges in $L^1$. This proves local absolute continuity in time and the asserted almost-everywhere equations. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-diffusion"></a>
<div id="lem:diffusion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gradient-martingale"></a>
<a id="eq:qprime"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Diffusion and square-martingale identity). *For $\gamma\in\mathcal U$, the equation <a id="eq:controlled-diffusion"></a>$$\mathrm dX_t=\gamma(t)u_\gamma(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt+\mathrm dB_t,
 \qquad X_0=0,
 \label{eq:controlled-diffusion}$$ has a unique strong solution on $[0,1]$. On every strip $[0,T]$ with $T<1$, $$u_\gamma(t,X_t)=\int_0^t
                \Phi_{\gamma,xx}(s,X_s)\,\mathrm dB_s.
 \label{eq:gradient-martingale}$$ In particular, $q(t)=\mathbb E[u_\gamma(t,X_t)^2]$ belongs to $C^1[0,1)$, $q(0)=0$, and $$q'(t)=\mathbb E[\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(t,X_t)^2].
 \label{eq:qprime}$$ Under the approximations in Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>, the coupled diffusions converge uniformly on compact time strips, and $q_n$ and $q_n'$ converge uniformly there.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-diffusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-diffusion</summary>

*Proof.* The drift is measurable in time, bounded by $\gamma(t)$, and globally Lipschitz in space on every compact time strip by Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>. Picard iteration gives a unique strong solution there. These solutions agree on overlaps and extend to time one because $\int_0^1\gamma<\infty$ and Brownian paths are continuous.

For clarity, convergence needs no moment estimate for the approximating paths. Couple them using the same Brownian motion. The drift difference is bounded by a common spatial Lipschitz constant times $|X_n-X|$, plus $|\gamma_n-\gamma|+\gamma_n\|u_n-u_\gamma\|_\infty$. Gronwall’s inequality gives a deterministic bound for $\sup_{t\leq T}|X_n(t)-X(t)|$ tending to zero. For step coefficients and smooth terminal data, Itô’s formula on each step cancels the drift of $u_n(t,X_n(t))$. The step-boundary values agree. Passing to the limit in the stochastic integral by its $L^2$ isometry proves <a href="#eq:gradient-martingale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-martingale">[eq:gradient-martingale]</a>. Its initial value is zero by evenness. The same isometry gives $q(t)=\int_0^t\mathbb E[\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(s,X_s)^2]\,\mathrm ds$. The integrand is continuous by joint continuity, boundedness on compact strips, and continuity of $X$. This proves <a href="#eq:qprime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:qprime">[eq:qprime]</a> and the stated convergence assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-constant-transfer"></a>
<div id="lem:constant-transfer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Transfer on a constant interval). *Suppose $\gamma=c$ on an open interval $I\subset(0,1)$. If $c>0$, every point of $I$ has a neighborhood on which one may choose positive, bounded, nondecreasing finite step approximants equal to $c$, with smooth terminal data and $M_n\geq\|\gamma_n\|_\infty$. At each time in this neighborhood, all spatial derivatives and the corresponding diffusion expectations of bounded polynomials in those derivatives converge. Moreover, $\Phi_{\gamma,xx}>0$ throughout $I\times\mathbb R$.*

<a id="eq:first-curvature"></a>
<a id="eq:second-curvature"></a>
*For $c>0$, set $r=c\Phi_x$, $v=r_x$, $w=r_{xx}$, and $z=r_{xxx}$. Then, within $I$, $$\begin{aligned}
 (q')'(t)&=c^{-2}\mathbb E[w(t,X_t)^2-2v(t,X_t)^3],
 \label{eq:first-curvature}\\
 (q')''(t)&=c^{-2}\mathbb E[z(t,X_t)^2-12v(t,X_t)w(t,X_t)^2
                                      +6v(t,X_t)^4].
 \label{eq:second-curvature}
\end{aligned}$$ For $c=0$, $q'$ is strictly increasing on $I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-constant-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-constant-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* To preserve a positive plateau, truncate and discretize the coefficient separately to the left and right of a closed subinterval of $I$, retain the value $c$ on that subinterval, and impose a positive lower cutoff tending to zero and smaller than $c$. All choices are monotone and converge in $L^1$. Increase the terminal smoothing parameter beyond the finite step bound. The convergence claims then follow from Lemmas <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a> and <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a>. The products involved are uniformly bounded, and the spatial derivative bounds also give uniform continuity for evaluating them on the coupled paths.

For strict positivity, choose $b\in I$ later than the time in question and use <a href="#eq:constant-heat" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constant-heat">[eq:constant-heat]</a>. The function $h=\Phi_\gamma(b,\cdot)$ is smooth, convex, even, and differs from $|x|$ by a bounded function. Its derivative is therefore not constant. Twice differentiating the logarithm in <a href="#eq:constant-heat" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constant-heat">[eq:constant-heat]</a> gives $$\Phi_{xx}(t,x)=\mathbb E_{t,x}^{\rm tilt}[h'']
                     +c\operatorname{Var}_{t,x}^{\rm tilt}(h').$$ The tilted Gaussian law has a strictly positive density everywhere. The variance is strictly positive and the first term is nonnegative.

Inside $I$ all functions are smooth in time as well as space, by the heat formula. Along $X$, the drifts of $r$, $v$, and $w$ are respectively $0$, $-v^2$, and $-3vw$, and their diffusion coefficients are $v$, $w$, and $z$. These identities follow by differentiating $r_t=-r_{xx}/2-rr_x$. Itô’s formula for $v^2$ and then for $w^2-2v^3$, with <a href="#eq:qprime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:qprime">[eq:qprime]</a>, proves <a href="#eq:first-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-curvature">[eq:first-curvature]</a>–<a href="#eq:second-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:second-curvature">[eq:second-curvature]</a>. All differentiations under the expectation are justified by the bounded higher spatial derivatives on smaller intervals.

If $c=0$, the same argument gives $$(q')'(t)=\mathbb E[\Phi_{xxx}(t,X_t)^2]>0.$$ To see strictness, start the drift-free interval at any earlier time in $I$. Its Gaussian transition kernel implies that $X_t$ has a strictly positive density on $\mathbb R$. The function $\Phi_{xxx}(t,\cdot)$ cannot vanish identically: otherwise $\Phi(t,\cdot)$ would be an even quadratic polynomial, whereas it is one-Lipschitz and differs from $|x|$ by a bounded function. ◻

</details>

<a id="shape:section"></a>
### Shape inequalities for finite step functions

On a positive constant interval, the curvature formula <a href="#eq:second-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:second-curvature">[eq:second-curvature]</a> contains the indefinite expression $z^2-12vw^2+6v^4$. The next section will make its expectation positive by adding a spatial derivative of zero expectation. Our task here is to prove the shape inequalities that make this possible: we compare the backward drift with a logarithmic derivative of the forward density.

We work with a fixed positive finite step coefficient and a smooth terminal condition. The estimates in this section may depend on these fixed data. Section <a href="#sec:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curvature">5</a> will pass only the resulting integrated inequality to general coefficients, using Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>.

<a id="shape:backward-r"></a>
<a id="tail:g-jump"></a>
Fix a partition $0=t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_N=1$, constants $0<c_1\le\cdots\le c_N\le M$, and $\gamma(t)=c_i$ on $[t_{i-1},t_i)$. Let $\Phi$ solve $$\Phi_t=-\frac12(\Phi_{xx}+\gamma\Phi_x^2),
 \qquad \Phi(1,x)=M^{-1}\log(2\cosh(Mx)).$$ On a step where $\gamma=c$, put $$l=c\Phi,\qquad r=l_x,\qquad v=r_x,
 \qquad w=r_{xx},\qquad z=r_{xxx}.$$ Spatial derivatives of $\Phi$ agree across step boundaries; those of $l$ are rescaled with $c$. Within a step, $h=e^l$ satisfies $h_t=-\tfrac12h_{xx}$. In backward time $\tau$ this gives $$\label{shape:backward-r}
 r_\tau=\frac12r_{xx}+rr_x.$$ Let $X_0=0$ and $\mathrm dX_t=r(t,X_t)\mathrm dt+\mathrm dB_t$. The drift is bounded and spatially Lipschitz for these fixed data. Its density at positive times will be denoted by $f(t,x)$. Factor it as $$f=e^l g,\qquad s=-\partial_x\log g.$$ The factor $e^l$ contains the backward solution. The remaining factor $g$ evolves by the forward heat equation within each step. Indeed, the transition density from time $a$ to time $t$ in the same step is $$p_{t-a}(x-y)\frac{h(t,x)}{h(a,y)},\qquad
 p_u(x)=(2\pi u)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/(2u)}.$$ It integrates to one because $h(a,\cdot)=P_{t-a}h(t,\cdot)$, and differentiation verifies the forward equation with drift $r=h_x/h$. Consequently $g_t=\tfrac12g_{xx}$. On the first step, $g(t,x)=p_t(x)/h(0,0)$, so $s=x/t$. At a forward jump $c_+-c_-=\delta\ge0$, continuity of $f$ gives $$\label{tail:g-jump}
 g_+=g_-e^{-\delta\Phi}.$$ In particular $g$ is positive and even, and $s$ is odd.

The comparison we need has three parts. On the positive half-line the drift $r$ is increasing and concave; its relative backward-time change $r_\tau/r$ is nonpositive and nondecreasing in $x$. The score $s$ is also increasing and concave, and its ratio to $r$ is nondecreasing in $x$. Lemmas <a href="#shape:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:backward">6</a> and <a href="#shape:forward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:forward">7</a> state these claims precisely. We first record the boundary estimates needed in their proofs.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Boundary estimates and comparison

A differentiated exponential error means that, for each fixed finite derivative order, the error and all its derivatives up to that order are $O(e^{-\kappa x})$ as $x\to+\infty$, for some $\kappa>0$. Constants may change with the derivative order.

<a id="stmt-tail-class"></a>
<div id="tail:class" class="lemma">

<a id="tail:backward"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Differentiated tails). *On each closed backward step, uniformly in its time coordinate, $$\label{tail:backward}
 l(t,x)=cx+C(t)+O(e^{-\kappa x})\qquad(x\to+\infty)$$ with differentiated exponential error. The functions are even, so the negative tail is determined by symmetry.*

<a id="tail:forward"></a>
<a id="tail:score"></a>
*For the forward heat factor $g$, on every closed forward step with time bounded away from zero, $$\label{tail:forward}
 g(t,x)=\exp\left(-\frac{x^2}{2t}+\beta(t)x+C_0(t)\right)
                 (1+O(e^{-\kappa x}))\qquad(x\to+\infty),$$ with differentiated relative error. In particular, $$\label{tail:score}
 s(t,x)=\frac{x}{t}-\beta(t)+O(e^{-\kappa x}),\qquad
 s_x(t,x)=\frac1t+O(e^{-\kappa x}),\qquad
 s_{xx}(t,x)=O(e^{-\kappa x}).$$ The assertions include one-sided values at every step boundary.*

</div>

The proof uses only Gaussian convolution and the finite-step jump rules; none of the shape inequalities proved below enters it.

<details id="proof-stmt-tail-class"><summary>Proof of stmt-tail-class</summary>

<a id="tail:gaussian-estimate"></a>
*Proof.* Here is a Gaussian estimate that also controls differentiation. Suppose $q\in C^m(\mathbb R)$ satisfies, for $0\le j\le m$, $$|q^{(j)}(y)|\le A e^{-\kappa y}\quad(y\ge0),\qquad
 |q^{(j)}(y)|\le A e^{K|y|}\quad(y<0).$$ For $Y_x=\lambda x+b+\sqrt V Z$, where $Z$ is standard normal, $0<\lambda_*\le\lambda\le\lambda^*$, $|b|\le B$, and $0\le V\le V_*$, $$\label{tail:gaussian-estimate}
 \partial_x^j\mathbb E q(Y_x)
       =\lambda^j\mathbb E q^{(j)}(Y_x)=O(e^{-\kappa' x})
       \qquad(0\le j\le m)$$ uniformly in these parameters, for some $\kappa'>0$. Indeed, write $\mu=\lambda x+b$ and take $x$ large enough that $\mu>0$. On $Y_x\ge\mu/2$, use $Ae^{-\kappa\mu/2}$. On its complement, the global bound $|q^{(j)}(y)|\le A e^{K|y|}$, Cauchy–Schwarz, and $\mathbb P(Y_x<\mu/2)\le e^{-\mu^2/(8V_*)}$ give a bound $C\exp(K\mu-\mu^2/(16V_*))$. This is smaller than an exponential in $x$; the case $V=0$ is immediate. Exponential integrability of the Gaussian justifies differentiation under the expectation. Adjusting the constant covers the remaining bounded range of $x$.

For backward evolution, suppose an even starting function $l_0$ has $l_0(x)=cx+C+\rho(x)$ with differentiated exponential error. Set $q(y)=e^{l_0(y)-cy-C}-1$. This $q$ obeys the hypotheses above: the positive tail follows by differentiating the exponential, and evenness of $l_0$ gives at most exponential growth for $q$ and its derivatives on the negative tail. Gaussian tilting gives $$P_\tau(e^{l_0})(x)
 =e^{cx+C+c^2\tau/2}
   \left(1+\mathbb E q(x+c\tau+\sqrt\tau Z)\right).$$ Apply <a href="#tail:gaussian-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:gaussian-estimate">[tail:gaussian-estimate]</a> uniformly for bounded $\tau$ and take the logarithm. Its relative error tends uniformly to zero, so differentiating $\log(1+\cdot)$ proves <a href="#tail:backward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:backward">[tail:backward]</a> with all the stated derivatives. The initial condition starts this induction since, for $x>0$, $$\frac cM\log(2\cosh Mx)
       =cx+\frac cM\log(1+e^{-2Mx}).$$ At a backward boundary, $l$ is multiplied by $c_-/c_+>0$, which preserves the required tail class. Finite induction proves the first claim.

Suppose at a positive time $a$ that $g(a,y)=e^{-y^2/(2a)+\beta y+C}(1+q(y))$ has the required tail class. Evenness of $g$ implies the same exponential-growth bound for the derivatives of $q$ on the negative tail. Completing the square gives $$\begin{aligned}
 P_hg(a,\cdot)(x)
 &=\sqrt{\frac a{a+h}}\,
   \exp\left(-\frac{x^2}{2(a+h)}
      +\frac{a\beta}{a+h}x+C+\frac{\beta^2ah}{2(a+h)}\right)\\
 &\quad\times
   \left(1+\mathbb E q\left(
        \frac a{a+h}x+\frac{\beta ah}{a+h}
           +\sqrt{\frac{ah}{a+h}}Z\right)\right).
\end{aligned}$$ On a fixed interval $0\le h\le H$, the coefficient of $x$ in the Gaussian mean is at least $a/(a+H)>0$. Thus <a href="#tail:gaussian-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:gaussian-estimate">[tail:gaussian-estimate]</a> proves closure, uniformly including $h=0$. At a jump, write $\Phi=x+D+\rho$ on the positive tail. Formula <a href="#tail:g-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:g-jump">[tail:g-jump]</a> replaces $(\beta,C)$ by $(\beta-\delta,C-\delta D)$ and replaces $1+q$ by $(1+q)e^{-\delta\rho}$, preserving the differentiated relative error. Finite induction proves <a href="#tail:forward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:forward">[tail:forward]</a>. Logarithmic differentiation then gives <a href="#tail:score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:score">[tail:score]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="shape:barrier"></a>
We will use the following elementary form of comparison. If on a compact space-time cylinder $$u_t-\tfrac12u_{xx}-b u_x-c u\ge0,\qquad c\le C,$$ and $u\ge0$ initially while $u\ge-\varepsilon$ on the lateral boundary, then $$\label{shape:barrier}
 u(t,x)\ge-\varepsilon e^{(\max(C,0)+1)(t-t_0)}.$$ To see this, add the positive exponential on the right to $u$ and multiply by $e^{-(\max(C,0)+1)(t-t_0)}$. The resulting zeroth-order coefficient is at most $-1$. A negative space-time minimum, which cannot be on the parabolic boundary, has nonpositive time derivative, zero spatial gradient, and nonnegative second spatial derivative, contradicting its differential inequality. The same proof applies in a ball in $\mathbb R^d$ with $\tfrac12\Delta$ in place of $\tfrac12\partial_{xx}$. In particular, to exhaust the line or half-line it suffices that the lateral boundary errors tend uniformly to zero and that $C$ is independent of the truncation. No uniform bound on the first-order coefficient $b$ over the exhausting cylinders is needed.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Backward shape inequalities

<a id="stmt-shape-backward"></a>
<div id="shape:backward" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Backward shapes). *For the fixed step data above, $r$ is odd and $v>0$. On $x>0$, <a id="shape:backward-signs"></a>$$\label{shape:backward-signs}
 w\le0,\qquad
 H:=\frac{r_\tau}{r}=\frac{w}{2r}+v\le0,
 \qquad H_x\ge0.$$ The quotient defining $H$ extends smoothly and evenly at zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shape-backward"><summary>Proof of stmt-shape-backward</summary>

*Proof.* Evenness is preserved by both heat evolution and rescaling. If $l_0''>0$, the logarithm of its heat evolution obeys $$\partial_{xx}\log P_\tau e^{l_0}(x)
 =\mathbb E_\nu l_0''(Y)
     +\operatorname{Var}_\nu(l_0'(Y))>0,$$ where $\nu$ is the Gaussian law of $Y=x+\sqrt\tau Z$ tilted by $e^{l_0(Y)}$; at $\tau=0$ use $l_0''(x)>0$ directly. Since the terminal second derivative is $cM\operatorname{sech}^2(Mx)>0$ and backward rescalings are positive, this proves $v>0$. Thus $r(x)>0$ for $x>0$. All required derivatives are bounded on closed steps, by smoothness on compact sets and Lemma <a href="#tail:class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tail:class">5</a> at infinity.

Differentiating <a href="#shape:backward-r" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:backward-r">[shape:backward-r]</a> twice gives $$w_\tau=\tfrac12w_{xx}+r w_x+3v w.$$ The terminal $w=-2cM^2\operatorname{sech}^2(Mx)\tanh(Mx)$ is nonpositive for $x>0$. The lateral values are $w(\tau,0)=0$ and $w(\tau,A)=o_A(1)$ uniformly on a closed step. Apply <a href="#shape:barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:barrier">[shape:barrier]</a> to $-w$, with $C=3\sup v$, and let $A\to\infty$. Backward jumps multiply $w$ by a positive constant, so the sign holds throughout.

<a id="shape:H-evolution"></a>
Because $r(x)=v(0)x+O(x^3)$ with $v(0)>0$, odd smoothness makes $w/r$ smooth and even at zero. Direct differentiation of $r_\tau=rH$ gives <a id="shape:Hx-evolution"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 H_\tau&=\tfrac12H_{xx}+(v/r+r)H_x+vH,
     \label{shape:H-evolution}\\
 (H_x)_\tau&=\tfrac12(H_x)_{xx}+(v/r+r)(H_x)_x
       +\bigl((v/r)_x+2v\bigr)H_x+wH.
     \label{shape:Hx-evolution}
\end{aligned}$$ At the terminal endpoint of the last step, $$H=(cM-M^2)\operatorname{sech}^2(Mx)\le0,
 \qquad H_x\ge0\quad(x>0).$$ For <a href="#shape:H-evolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:H-evolution">[shape:H-evolution]</a>, write $v/r+r=x^{-1}+\eta(x)$, where $\eta$ is smooth odd and $\eta(x)=O(x)$ near zero. The radial lift $\widehat H(y)=H(|y|)$ in $\mathbb R^3$ satisfies $$\widehat H_\tau=\tfrac12\Delta\widehat H
       +\eta(|y|)\frac y{|y|}\cdot\nabla\widehat H+v(|y|)\widehat H.$$ Both the lifted function and vector field are smooth at zero: a smooth even function lifts smoothly, and $\eta(x)/x$ is smooth even. Since $H\to0$ uniformly at infinity, comparison on balls followed by exhaustion gives $H\le0$.

For $H_x$, use intervals $[\varepsilon,A]$. The source $wH$ is nonnegative, and $$(v/r)_x=\frac{w}{r}-\frac{v^2}{r^2}<0,
 \qquad (v/r)_x+2v\le2\sup v.$$ The lateral errors are $H_x(\tau,\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon)$, uniformly by smooth evenness, and $H_x(\tau,A)=o_A(1)$ by the tails. Formula <a href="#shape:barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:barrier">[shape:barrier]</a>, with a bound independent of $\varepsilon,A$, proves $H_x\ge0$ on taking the two limits.

Finally, at a backward jump write $r_{\rm new}=\alpha r_{\rm old}$, where $0<\alpha\le1$. Its derivatives rescale identically, and hence $$H_{\rm new}=H_{\rm old}-(1-\alpha)v_{\rm old},\qquad
 (H_x)_{\rm new}=(H_x)_{\rm old}-(1-\alpha)w_{\rm old}.$$ These preserve both signs. Induction over the finitely many steps completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Forward score inequalities

The logarithmic derivative $s=-g_x/g$ measures the part of the density not contained in $e^l$. We need both its concavity and a comparison with $r$, rather than concavity of either function alone. The density transform and its use on constant intervals also appear in Lopatto’s positive-temperature analysis (Lopatto 2026, secs. 3, 7, and 9) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Lopatto2026](#ref-Lopatto2026)]. Here we prove the additional ratio sign needed by the coercive identity.

<a id="stmt-shape-forward"></a>
<div id="shape:forward" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Forward score and ratio). *For every $t>0$, $s$ is odd and, with $a=s_x$, <a id="shape:forward-signs"></a>$$\label{shape:forward-signs}
 a\ge\frac1t>0,\qquad s_{xx}\le0\quad(x>0),\qquad
 j:=a-\frac sr v=r\partial_x(s/r)\ge0\quad(x\in\mathbb R).$$ The ratios and $j$ extend smoothly at zero. At every forward step boundary, $j$ is unchanged.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shape-forward"><summary>Proof of stmt-shape-forward</summary>

<a id="shape:a-evolution"></a>
<a id="shape:sxx-evolution"></a>
<a id="shape:s-jump"></a>
*Proof.* Evenness and positivity of $g$ give odd smoothness of $s$. Within a step, the heat equation for $g$ gives <a id="shape:s-evolution"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 s_t&=\tfrac12s_{xx}-s s_x,
       \label{shape:s-evolution}\\
 a_t&=\tfrac12a_{xx}-s a_x-a^2,
       \label{shape:a-evolution}\\
 (s_{xx})_t&=\tfrac12(s_{xx})_{xx}
                  -s(s_{xx})_x-3a s_{xx}.
       \label{shape:sxx-evolution}
\end{aligned}$$ On the first interval $s=x/t$, so the first two claims hold explicitly. At a jump of size $\delta=c_+-c_-$, <a href="#tail:g-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:g-jump">[tail:g-jump]</a> gives $$\label{shape:s-jump}
 s_+=s_-+\delta\Phi_x,
 \quad a_+=a_-+\delta\Phi_{xx},
 \quad (s_{xx})_+=(s_{xx})_-+\delta\Phi_{xxx}.$$ Here $\Phi_{xx}>0$ and $\Phi_{xxx}\le0$ on the positive half-line by Lemma <a href="#shape:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:backward">6</a>.

For subsequent intervals, put $u=a-1/t$. Equation <a href="#shape:a-evolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:a-evolution">[shape:a-evolution]</a> becomes the linear equation $$u_t=\tfrac12u_{xx}-s u_x-(a+1/t)u.$$ For the fixed smooth solution, $a$ is bounded on a closed step; this follows from <a href="#tail:score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:score">[tail:score]</a> and compactness and does not presume its sign. Thus the zeroth-order coefficient is bounded above independently of a spatial truncation. Initially $u\ge0$, by the preceding step and <a href="#shape:s-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:s-jump">[shape:s-jump]</a>; at both infinities $u\to0$ uniformly by Lemma <a href="#tail:class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tail:class">5</a>. Comparison on $[-A,A]$ and exhaustion prove $a\ge1/t$. Next apply comparison to $-s_{xx}$ on $[0,A]$ using <a href="#shape:sxx-evolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:sxx-evolution">[shape:sxx-evolution]</a>. Its zeroth coefficient is $-3a\le0$, its initial values are nonnegative, and its boundary values tend to zero, at zero by oddness and at infinity by <a href="#tail:score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:score">[tail:score]</a>. This proves $s_{xx}\le0$.

For the ratio inequality, first note that $s(x)>0$ and $r(x)>0$ for $x>0$. On this half-line define $$d_s=\frac{s_x}{s},\qquad d_r=\frac{r_x}{r},\qquad \rho=d_s-d_r.$$ Then $j=s\rho$, so it suffices to prove $\rho\ge0$. On the first step, $s=x/t$ and the concavity of $r$ gives $r(x)=\int_0^x v(y)\mathrm dy\ge xv(x)$; hence $\rho=1/x-v/r\ge0$. Within any later step, forward time reverses <a href="#shape:backward-r" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:backward-r">[shape:backward-r]</a>, so $r_t=-rH$. Consequently $$(d_s)_t=\tfrac12(d_s)_{xx}+d_s(d_s)_x-s_{xx},
 \qquad (d_r)_t=-H_x.$$ Also $\tfrac12(d_r)_{xx}+d_r(d_r)_x
 =\tfrac12(w/r)_x=H_x-w$. Subtracting gives <a id="shape:G-evolution"></a>$$\label{shape:G-evolution}
 \rho_t=\tfrac12\rho_{xx}+d_s\rho_x+(d_r)_x\rho
                         +2H_x-w-s_{xx}.$$ The source is nonnegative by the signs already proved, while $(d_r)_x=w/r-v^2/r^2<0$.

Here are the endpoint details for this last comparison. On a closed step away from time zero, $v(t,0)>0$ is continuous and bounded below, and $a(t,0)\ge1/t>0$. Odd Taylor expansions therefore give, uniformly, $$d_s=x^{-1}+O(x),\qquad d_r=x^{-1}+O(x),\qquad \rho=O(x)
       \quad(x\downarrow0).$$ At infinity, <a href="#tail:backward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:backward">[tail:backward]</a> and <a href="#tail:score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:score">[tail:score]</a> give $d_r=O(e^{-\kappa x})$ and $d_s=(x-\beta(t)t)^{-1}+O(e^{-\kappa' x})$, so $\rho\to0$ uniformly. Use <a href="#shape:barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:barrier">[shape:barrier]</a> on $[\varepsilon,A]$, with upper zeroth-order bound zero, and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, $A\to\infty$. This proves $\rho\ge0$ provided it is nonnegative at the start of the step.

To verify that condition, write at a forward jump $$r_+=\alpha r_-,\quad v_+=\alpha v_-,\quad
 s_+=s_-+\lambda r_-,\quad a_+=a_-+\lambda v_-,
 \qquad
 \alpha=\frac{c_+}{c_-}>0,\quad\lambda=\frac{c_+-c_-}{c_-}\ge0.$$ Then direct cancellation gives $$j_+=a_-+\lambda v_-
     -\frac{s_-+\lambda r_-}{\alpha r_-}\alpha v_-=j_-.$$ Since $\rho_+=j_+/s_+$ for $x>0$, its initial sign is preserved. This proves the ratio claim by induction. Smoothness of $s/r$ at zero follows by factoring $x$ from both odd functions; in particular $j$ is even and $j(0)=0$. Reflection gives the assertion on the whole line. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Variables for the integral certificate

We now express the shape inequalities as nonnegative quantities suited to polynomial algebra. Set $$R=r^2,\quad K=rs,\quad b=-w/r,\quad
 j=s_x-(s/r)v=r(s/r)_x.$$ The first two quantities are nonnegative by the common signs of $r$ and $s$, and $b\ge0$ expresses the concavity of $r$. The ratio comparison is precisely $j\ge0$. The remaining three quantities encode the backward-time and forward-concavity inequalities: $$B=b-2v=-2H,\qquad
 S=z+bv-2Rb=2rH_x,\qquad L=-r s_{xx}.$$ All are nonnegative. The following lemma collects their derivative rules and the integration-by-parts identity that will be used in the coercive calculation.

<a id="stmt-shape-ibp"></a>
<div id="shape:ibp" class="lemma">

<a id="shape:D-rules"></a>
<a id="shape:ibp-identity"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Certificate signs and integration by parts). *At a fixed positive time in a constant step, the functions $R,K,v,b,j,L,B,S$ defined above are even and extend smoothly across zero. They satisfy $$R,K,v,j,L,B,S\ge0.$$ Writing $\mathcal D=r\partial_x$, one has $$\label{shape:D-rules}
 \mathcal D(R,K,v,b,j)
  =\bigl(2Rv,\ 2vK+Rj,\ -Rb,\ -z-bv,\ -jv+Kb-L\bigr).$$ For every polynomial $F$ in $R,K,v,b,j$, $$\label{shape:ibp-identity}
 \mathbb E_f\!\left[\mathcal DF+(v+R-K)F\right]=0.$$ Every term in this identity is absolutely integrable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-shape-ibp"><summary>Proof of stmt-shape-ibp</summary>

*Proof.* The signs of $R,K,v,j,L$ follow from Lemmas <a href="#shape:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:backward">6</a> and <a href="#shape:forward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:forward">7</a>; for $K$ use the common sign of the odd functions $r,s$. Since $H=v-b/2$, we have $B=-2H\ge0$. On $x>0$, differentiation gives $$S=2rH_x\ge0.$$ All these expressions are even. The ratios $w/r$ and $s/r$ are smooth at zero since their numerators and denominator are odd and $r_x(0)>0$. The inequalities consequently extend there by continuity. For example $b(0)=-z(0)/v(0)$, $j(0)=0$, and $S(0)=0$.

The derivative rules follow directly from the definitions. The only one involving the forward score is $$rj_x=r s_{xx}-a v-sw+\frac{s v^2}{r}
     =-jv+Kb-L,$$ which gives the last rule in <a href="#shape:D-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:D-rules">[shape:D-rules]</a>.

The density has logarithmic derivative $f_x/f=r-s$. Therefore $$\partial_x(rFf)
   =\left(\mathcal DF+(v+r(r-s))F\right)f
   =\left(\mathcal DF+(v+R-K)F\right)f.$$ By the differentiated tails, $r$ is bounded and tends to $c$ at the positive tail, $v,w,z,b$ and their derivatives decay exponentially, $s=x/t+O(1)$, $j=1/t+O((1+x)e^{-\kappa x})$, and $K=O(1+x)$. Every polynomial $F$ and its differentiated expression thus have at most polynomial growth. Moreover $$f(t,x)\le C\exp\left(-\frac{x^2}{2t}+C|x|\right).$$ These bounds prove absolute integrability and $rFf\to0$ at both infinities. Integrating the displayed derivative on $[-A,A]$ and letting $A\to\infty$ proves <a href="#shape:ibp-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:ibp-identity">[shape:ibp-identity]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="cert:section"></a>
### An exact coercive identity

<a id="cert:signs"></a>
We retain the fixed positive time, positive constant step, and notation of Lemma <a href="#shape:ibp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:ibp">8</a>. We will prove $$\mathbb E_f[z^2-12vw^2+6v^4]\geq\frac1{1000}\mathbb E_fv^4.$$ The expression need not be positive pointwise. Subtracting a spatial derivative with zero expectation preserves its expectation; the calculation below decomposes the resulting expression into nonnegative terms. The variables introduced at the end of the previous section satisfy $$\label{cert:signs}
 R,K,v,B,j,L,S\geq0,\qquad b=B+2v\geq0,
 \qquad Rb^2=w^2.$$ In particular, the signs of $B$ and $S$ record the backward inequalities $H\leq0$ and $H_x\geq0$.

<a id="cert:divergence"></a>
For a polynomial $F$ in $R,K,v,b,j$, set <a id="cert:operators"></a>$$\label{cert:operators}
 \mathcal D F=r\partial_xF,\qquad
 \mathcal W(F)=\mathcal DF+(v+R-K)F.$$ The derivative rules in <a href="#shape:D-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:D-rules">[shape:D-rules]</a> determine $\mathcal W$ algebraically. Its analytic meaning is $$\label{cert:divergence}
 f\mathcal W(F)=\partial_x(rfF),\qquad
 \mathbb E_f\mathcal W(F)=0.$$ Lemma <a href="#shape:ibp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:ibp">8</a> supplies the integrability and vanishing boundary terms for every polynomial used here. Thus it suffices to find $F$ for which subtracting $\mathcal W(F)$ from the curvature expression leaves a positive multiple of $v^4$ and other nonnegative terms.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Choosing the derivative correction

We first arrange the terms containing $z$. Set <a id="cert:constants-first"></a>$$\label{cert:constants-first}
 (A,C,k,T_0,g_0,h)
 =\left(\frac1{13},\frac{53}{24},\frac{19}{16},\frac{73}{48},
         \frac{2331}{10000},\frac{87}{2500}\right)$$ and define <a id="cert:QT"></a>$$\label{cert:QT}
 Q=Avb+Cv^2+kKv-2kRb+T_0Rv,
 \qquad T=g_0Rv+hjv.$$ The terms $(z+Q)^2$ and $ST$ will be nonnegative: the first is a square, and $S,T\geq0$. Since $S=z+bv-2Rb$, their terms linear in $z$ have total coefficient $2Q+T$.

The only derivative rule containing $z$ is $\mathcal Db=-z-bv$. Consequently the coefficient of $z$ in $\mathcal W(F)$ is $-\partial_bF$. We therefore choose $F=F_2$ so that $\partial_bF_2=2Q+T$. Set <a id="cert:constants-second"></a>$$\label{cert:constants-second}
 (x_0,y,m,n_0)
 =\left(\frac{4663}{5000},\frac{6853}{10000},
          -\frac{1051}{400},-\frac{1073}{500}\right).$$ The following polynomial has the required derivative: <a id="cert:polynomials"></a>$$\label{cert:polynomials}
\begin{aligned}
 F_2={}&(Av-2kR)b^2\\
       &+b\bigl(2Cv^2+2kKv+(g_0+2T_0)Rv+hjv\bigr)\\
       &+x_0v^2j+yv^3+mKv^2+n_0Rv^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Its first two lines are the required antiderivative in $b$; the last line is independent of $b$ and will be used in the remaining terms. Directly, $$\partial_bF_2
 =2Avb-4kRb+2Cv^2+2kKv+(g_0+2T_0)Rv+hjv
 =2Q+T.$$ It follows that $$z^2-12Rvb^2+6v^4-(z+Q)^2-ST-\mathcal W(F_2)$$ has no $z$ dependence. This is the reason for the choice of the derivative correction.

<a id="cert:L-correction"></a>
The remaining calculation uses one further favorable term from the forward score inequality. Let $\mathcal W_0$ be the polynomial rule obtained from $\mathcal W$ by omitting $-L$ in $\mathcal Dj=-jv+Kb-L$. Since $$\label{cert:L-correction}
 \partial_jF_2=x_0v^2+hbv,
 \qquad
 \mathcal W(F_2)=\mathcal W_0(F_2)-L(x_0v^2+hbv),$$ replacing $\mathcal W$ by $\mathcal W_0$ leaves the nonnegative term $L(x_0v^2+hbv)$ to be retained in the final identity. We now have only a polynomial in $R,K,v,b,j$ to decompose.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The nonnegative remainder

Define the five polynomials <a id="cert:squares"></a>$$\label{cert:squares}
\begin{aligned}
 P_0&=Rj(b-7v)^2,\\
 P_1&=RB\left(b-\frac73v\right)^2,\\
 P_2&=Kv\left(b-\frac92v\right)^2,\\
 P_3&=vB\left(K+\frac14R-\frac17v\right)^2,\\
 P_4&=\left(vb-\frac{14}{5}v^2-\frac{13}{4}Kv+\frac43Rv\right)^2,
\end{aligned}$$ with positive coefficients <a id="cert:constants"></a>$$\label{cert:constants}
 (p_0,p_1,p_2,p_3,p_4)
 =\left(\frac{337}{10000},\frac{183}{2500},\frac{411}{10000},
         \frac{11461}{5000},\frac{647}{10000}\right).$$ Let $P_{\mathrm{rem}}$ be the polynomial in $R,K,v,B,j$ whose nonzero coefficients are listed in Table <a href="#cert:table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:table">1</a>. All its variables and coefficients are nonnegative.

<a id="stmt-cert-identity-lemma"></a>
<div id="cert:identity-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="cert:identity"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Rational polynomial identity). *With these definitions, $B=b-2v$, and $S=z+bv-2Rb$, one has the polynomial identity over $\mathbb Q$ $$\label{cert:identity}
\begin{aligned}
 z^2-12Rvb^2+6v^4
  ={}&(z+Q)^2+ST+\sum_{i=0}^4p_iP_i+\mathcal W(F_2)\\
     &+L(x_0v^2+hbv)+P_{\mathrm{rem}}.
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-identity-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-identity-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Equation <a href="#cert:L-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:L-correction">[cert:L-correction]</a> reduces the assertion to the same identity with $\mathcal W_0$ and without the displayed $L$ term. The construction of $F_2$ above cancels every term containing $z$. Expand the residual $$z^2-12Rvb^2+6v^4-(z+Q)^2-ST
       -\mathcal W_0(F_2)-\sum_{i=0}^4p_iP_i$$ using <a href="#shape:D-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:D-rules">[shape:D-rules]</a>, substitute $b=B+2v$, and collect monomials in $R,K,v,B,j$. The coefficients are exactly those of Table <a href="#cert:table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:table">1</a>, and every unlisted coefficient is zero. This proves the identity by rational arithmetic. ◻

</details>

<div id="cert:table">

| Monomial |        Coefficient | Monomial |          Coefficient |
|:--------:|-------------------:|:--------:|---------------------:|
| $R^2v^2$ |       $223/160000$ | $R^2vB$  |        $4007/240000$ |
| $R^2B^2$ |      $1097/120000$ | $RKv^2$  |           $83/80000$ |
|  $RKvB$  |       $3343/24000$ |  $Rv^3$  |     $16259/23400000$ |
| $Rv^2B$  |    $30179/5460000$ | $Rv^2j$  |             $26/625$ |
| $RvB^2$  |        $107/15000$ |  $RvBj$  |             $3/2500$ |
|  $RB^3$  |        $121/32500$ | $RB^2j$  |           $11/10000$ |
| $K^2v^2$ |        $579/20000$ | $K^2vB$  |           $207/2500$ |
|  $Kv^3$  |       $911/975000$ | $Kv^2B$  |         $349/420000$ |
| $Kv^2j$  |        $5011/5000$ | $KvB^2$  |         $133/130000$ |
|  $KvBj$  |          $87/2500$ |  $v^4$   | $1661507/1521000000$ |
|  $v^3B$  | $633799/621075000$ | $v^2B^2$ |      $10657/1690000$ |

All 22 nonzero coefficients of $P_{\mathrm{rem}}$; every unlisted coefficient is zero. Each listed coefficient is positive; the coefficient of $v^4$ gives the coercive lower bound.

</div>

<a id="stmt-cert-coercivity"></a>
<div id="cert:coercivity" class="proposition">

<a id="cert:inequality"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Integrated coercivity). *Suppose $r,v,w,z,s$ and the variables $R,K,b,j,L,B,S$ are related as in Lemma <a href="#shape:ibp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:ibp">8</a>, with the derivative rules <a href="#shape:D-rules" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shape:D-rules">[shape:D-rules]</a> and signs <a href="#cert:signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:signs">[cert:signs]</a>. Assume their quotients extend across zero. Let $f$ be a probability density satisfying $f_x/f=r-s$ such that every term of <a href="#cert:identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:identity">[cert:identity]</a> is integrable and $rfF_2$ tends to zero at both spatial infinities. Then $$\label{cert:inequality}
 \mathbb E_f[z^2-12vw^2+6v^4]
 \geq\frac{1661507}{1521000000}\mathbb E_fv^4
 \geq\frac1{1000}\mathbb E_fv^4.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-coercivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-coercivity</summary>

*Proof.* Integrating <a href="#cert:identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:identity">[cert:identity]</a> removes $\mathcal W(F_2)$ by <a href="#cert:divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:divergence">[cert:divergence]</a>. Every remaining term on the right is nonnegative. Indeed, the prefactors of the five squares $P_i$ are $Rj,RB,Kv,vB,1$; $T=v(g_0R+hj)\geq0$; and $L(x_0v^2+hbv)\geq0$. The table gives the stronger bound $$P_{\mathrm{rem}}\geq\frac{1661507}{1521000000}v^4,
 \qquad
 \frac{1661507}{1521000000}-\frac1{1000}
       =\frac{140507}{1521000000}>0.$$ Using $Rb^2=w^2$ proves <a href="#cert:inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:inequality">[cert:inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

For the smooth finite-step data of Section <a href="#shape:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:section">3</a>, Lemma <a href="#shape:ibp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:ibp">8</a> verifies all the hypotheses of this proposition. Although the proof uses the density score and its ratios, the resulting inequality involves only $v,w,z$. This is the form that passes to an integrable order parameter.

<a id="sec:curvature"></a>
### Strict curvature on constant intervals

We now pass from the smooth finite-step calculation to the order parameters in $\mathcal U$. Both sides of the final integrated inequality depend only on spatial derivatives of $\Phi$ through order four. The compact-strip bounds and convergence from Section <a href="#sec:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:regularity">2</a> therefore suffice to pass to the limit; no uniform tail constants or convergence of the forward scores are needed.

<a id="stmt-prop-gap-curvature"></a>
<div id="prop:gap-curvature" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:strict-curvature"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Constant-interval curvature). *Let $\gamma\in\mathcal U$ and suppose $\gamma=c$ on an open interval $I\subset(0,1)$. For its controlled diffusion, put $q(t)=\mathbb E\Phi_{\gamma,x}(t,X_t)^2$. If $c>0$, then $q'$ is strictly convex on $I$; more precisely, with $v=c\Phi_{\gamma,xx}$, $$\label{eq:strict-curvature}
 q'''(t)\geq\frac{1}{1000c^2}\mathbb Ev(t,X_t)^4>0,
 \qquad t\in I.$$ If $c=0$, then $q'$ is strictly increasing on $I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-gap-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-gap-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first $c>0$ and fix $t\in I$. Choose a closed neighborhood of $t$ inside $I$. Lemma <a href="#lem:constant-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:constant-transfer">4</a> supplies positive nondecreasing finite-step approximants $\gamma_n$, equal to $c$ there, and terminal smoothings $M_n^{-1}\log(2\cosh M_nx)$ with $M_n\geq\|\gamma_n\|_\infty$ and $M_n\to\infty$. Write $X^n$ for the associated diffusions and $r_n=c\Phi_{n,x}$, $v_n=r_{n,x}$, $w_n=r_{n,xx}$, $z_n=r_{n,xxx}$ on that neighborhood.

Lemmas <a href="#shape:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:backward">6</a>, <a href="#shape:forward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:forward">7</a>, and <a href="#shape:ibp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:ibp">8</a> verify all sign, integrability, and boundary hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#cert:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:coercivity">10</a>. Therefore $$\mathbb E[z_n^2-12v_nw_n^2+6v_n^4](t,X_t^n)
       \geq\frac1{1000}\mathbb Ev_n(t,X_t^n)^4.$$ The derivatives through order four converge uniformly in space and are uniformly bounded, by Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>. The coupled paths $X_t^n$ converge, and the next spatial derivative bounds ensure uniform continuity when evaluating at these paths. Thus each expectation in the display converges to its counterpart for $\gamma$. In particular no limit of $s_n$, $j_n$, or $w_n/r_n$ is needed.

Equation <a href="#eq:second-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:second-curvature">[eq:second-curvature]</a> identifies the resulting left side with $c^2q'''(t)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:constant-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:constant-transfer">4</a> also gives $v(t,x)=c\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(t,x)>0$ for every $x$, so its fourth moment is strictly positive. This proves <a href="#eq:strict-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-curvature">[eq:strict-curvature]</a> at every interior time, hence strict convexity of $q'$. The assertion for $c=0$ is the last part of Lemma <a href="#lem:constant-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:constant-transfer">4</a>; it is not obtained by dividing by $c$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:variation"></a>
### Full support and regularity

We now turn the constant-interval curvature into full support. The first variation must allow removal of arbitrary Stieltjes mass, not only removal of atoms: a minimizer need not initially be known to have a discrete or absolutely continuous measure. For related variational characterizations see Chen, Handschy, and Lerman (W.-K. Chen, Handschy, and Lerman 2018, Propositions 3–4) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CHL2018](#ref-CHL2018)] and Jagannath and Tobasco (Jagannath and Tobasco 2017, Lemma 3.2 and Corollary 3.6) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[JT2017](#ref-JT2017)].

<a id="stmt-lem-first-variation"></a>
<div id="lem:first-variation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:first-variation"></a>
**Lemma 12** (First variation in every admissible integrable direction). *Let $\gamma\in\mathcal U$, and suppose that $\eta\in L^1(0,1)$ has the property that $\gamma+\epsilon\eta\in\mathcal U$ for $0\leq\epsilon\leq\epsilon_0$, where $\epsilon_0>0$. With $X$ and $q$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a>, $$\left.\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\epsilon}\right|_{\epsilon=0+}
       \mathcal P(\gamma+\epsilon\eta)
   =\frac12\int_0^1\eta(t)(q(t)-t)\,\mathrm dt.
 \label{eq:first-variation}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-first-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-first-variation</summary>

<a id="eq:exact-difference"></a>
*Proof.* Put $\beta=\gamma+\epsilon\eta$, $u=\Phi_{\gamma,x}$, $\widetilde u=\Phi_{\beta,x}$, and $D=\Phi_\beta-\Phi_\gamma$. Subtracting the two equations gives $$D_t=-\tfrac12D_{xx}
       -\tfrac12\beta(\widetilde u+u)D_x
       -\tfrac12(\beta-\gamma)u^2.$$ Let $Y^\epsilon_0=0$ and $$\mathrm dY^\epsilon_t
   =\tfrac12\beta(t)(\widetilde u+u)(t,Y^\epsilon_t)\,\mathrm dt
      +\mathrm dB_t.$$ This diffusion exists uniquely by Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>; its drift is bounded by the integrable function $\beta$. Itô’s formula on $[0,T]$ yields the following identity. One may justify it without a classical time derivative by approximating $\gamma$ and $\beta$ separately by nondecreasing step functions, using the same smooth terminal datum for the pair. The two solutions, their spatial derivatives, and the associated linear diffusions converge on $[0,T]$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a> and the same Gronwall estimate as in Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a>. The source converges in $L^1$, since the gradients are bounded by one. Passing to the limit in their classical identities gives $$D(0,0)=\mathbb ED(T,Y^\epsilon_T)
       +\frac12\int_0^T(\beta-\gamma)(t)
                       \mathbb E[u(t,Y^\epsilon_t)^2]\,\mathrm dt.$$ The time-local form of <a href="#eq:value-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:value-stability">[eq:value-stability]</a> gives $\|D(T,\cdot)\|_\infty
 \leq\frac32\int_T^1|\beta-\gamma|$. Letting $T\uparrow1$ therefore proves the exact identity $$\frac{\Phi_\beta(0,0)-\Phi_\gamma(0,0)}\epsilon
    =\frac12\int_0^1\eta(t)
                     \mathbb E[u(t,Y^\epsilon_t)^2]\,\mathrm dt.
 \label{eq:exact-difference}$$

The coefficients $\beta$ have uniformly bounded $L^1$ norms for $0\leq\epsilon\leq\epsilon_0$, and converge to $\gamma$ in $L^1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a> gives uniform convergence $\widetilde u\to u$ and common spatial Lipschitz bounds on every compact strip. Coupling the diffusions with the same Brownian motion and using Gronwall’s inequality gives $Y^\epsilon\to X$ uniformly there. The integrand in <a href="#eq:exact-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-difference">[eq:exact-difference]</a> consequently converges for each $t<1$ to $\eta(t)q(t)$ and is bounded in absolute value by $|\eta(t)|$. Dominated convergence and differentiation of the linear penalty term in $\mathcal P$ prove <a href="#eq:first-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-variation">[eq:first-variation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-contact"></a>
<div id="lem:contact" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:contact-derivatives"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Contact conditions). *Let $\gamma$ minimize $\mathcal P$ over $\mathcal U$, and let $\mu$ be its locally finite Stieltjes measure on $[0,1)$, with the convention $\mu(\{0\})=\gamma(0)$. Thus $\gamma(t)=\mu([0,t])$. Define <a id="eq:contact-potential"></a>$$G(s)=\int_s^1(q(t)-t)\,\mathrm dt,
 \qquad S=\operatorname{supp}\mu\subset[0,1).
 \label{eq:contact-potential}$$ Then $G\geq0$ on $[0,1)$ and $G=0$ on $S$. At every $s\in S\cap(0,1)$, $$q(s)=s,\qquad q'(s)\leq1.
 \label{eq:contact-derivatives}$$ If $0\in S$, then also $q'(0)\leq1$, with the derivative from the right.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-contact"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-contact</summary>

*Proof.* For $s<1$, the direction $\eta=\mathbf1_{[s,1)}$ adds an atom to $\mu$ and is admissible. Minimality and Lemma <a href="#lem:first-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:first-variation">12</a> give $G(s)\geq0$. If $K\subset[0,1)$ is compact, let $\nu=\mu|_K$ and take $\eta_K(t)=-\nu([0,t])$. This bounded direction is admissible for $0\leq\epsilon\leq1$, since it replaces $\mu$ by $\mu-\epsilon\nu$. Fubini’s theorem gives $$0\leq D\mathcal P(\gamma)[\eta_K]
      =-\frac12\int_K G(s)\,\mu(\mathrm ds).$$ The interchange is legitimate because $|q(t)-t|\leq1$ and $\nu(K)<\infty$. Since $G\geq0$, it vanishes $\mu$-almost everywhere on every such $K$. Its continuity then gives $G=0$ on the relative support $S$.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a>, $G'(s)=s-q(s)$ and $G''(s)=1-q'(s)$ on compact strips. Every interior support point is a minimum of $G$, proving <a href="#eq:contact-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-derivatives">[eq:contact-derivatives]</a>. If $0\in S$, use $G(0)=0$ and $q(0)=0$ to write $$G(h)=\tfrac12(1-q'(0))h^2+o(h^2).$$ Nonnegativity for $h>0$ proves the asserted one-sided condition. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-no-terminal-plateau"></a>
<div id="lem:no-terminal-plateau" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (No terminal plateau). *For a minimizer $\gamma$, there is no $a<1$ and finite $c\geq0$ such that $\gamma=c$ on $(a,1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-no-terminal-plateau"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-no-terminal-plateau</summary>

<a id="eq:terminal-layer"></a>
*Proof.* The endpoint conclusion is recorded by Auffinger, Chen, and Zeng (Auffinger, Chen, and Zeng 2020, Remark 7) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[ACZ2017](#ref-ACZ2017)]. We give the quantitative argument needed here, including arbitrary positive constant plateaus. The boundary-layer method is also related to the zero-terminal-segment argument in El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke (El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke 2021, Lemma 6.12 and the proof of Theorem 5 in the cited preprint) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[EAMS2021](#ref-EAMS2021)]; their positivity-support statement concerns a different admissible class. Suppose such a plateau exists. We show that for some $C>0$, $$1-q(t)\geq C\sqrt{1-t}
 \quad\hbox{for all $t$ sufficiently close to one}.
 \label{eq:terminal-layer}$$ Fix $s\in(a,1)$, and let $\rho_s$ denote the law of $X_s$. Choose $R<\infty$ with $p_0=\rho_s([-R,R])>0$; no density assumption on $\rho_s$ is needed.

<a id="eq:terminal-kernel"></a>
When $c>0$, set $h(t,y)=\mathbb E\exp(c|y+\sqrt{1-t}Z|)$, with $Z$ standard normal. The heat formula gives $\Phi(t,y)=c^{-1}\log h(t,y)$ and drift $c\Phi_x=h_x/h$. Its transition density on the plateau is $$k_{s,t}(x,y)=p_{t-s}(y-x)\frac{h(t,y)}{h(s,x)},
 \qquad
 p_v(z)=\frac{e^{-z^2/(2v)}}{\sqrt{2\pi v}}.
 \label{eq:terminal-kernel}$$ Indeed, the heat-semigroup identity makes $h(t,B_t)/h(s,B_s)$ a positive mean-one martingale when Brownian motion is started at $(s,x)$. Its stochastic logarithm has the bounded coefficient $h_x/h=c\Phi_x$, so change of measure gives precisely this drift and kernel. For $c=0$ use the same kernel formula with $h\equiv1$.

<a id="eq:terminal-density"></a>
Put $L=1-s$ and $\delta=L/2$. Uniformly for $s+\delta\leq t<1$, $|x|\leq R$, and $|y|\leq1$, $$p_{t-s}(y-x)\geq
 (2\pi L)^{-1/2}e^{-(R+1)^2/(2\delta)}=:k_*>0,$$ while $h(t,y)\geq1$ and $h(s,x)\leq e^{cR}\mathbb Ee^{c\sqrt L|Z|}=:M$. Consequently the mixture of <a href="#eq:terminal-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:terminal-kernel">[eq:terminal-kernel]</a> against $\rho_s$ is a density $f_t$ satisfying $$f_t(y)\geq p_0k_*/M=:m>0
 \quad (s+\delta\leq t<1,\ |y|\leq1).
 \label{eq:terminal-density}$$

For $\epsilon=1-t$ and $|y|\leq\sqrt\epsilon$, differentiation of the terminal heat formula gives, also when $c=0$, $$u(t,y)=
 \frac{\mathbb E[\operatorname{sgn}(y+\sqrt\epsilon Z)
                      e^{c|y+\sqrt\epsilon Z|}]}
      {\mathbb Ee^{c|y+\sqrt\epsilon Z|}}.$$ For $\epsilon\leq1$ the denominator is at most $D=e^c\mathbb Ee^{c|Z|}<\infty$. The unnormalized mass of each sign is at least $p_*:=\Pr(Z\geq1)>0$, because $|y/\sqrt\epsilon|\leq1$ and the exponential tilt is at least one. Each sign thus has probability at least $\theta=p_*/D\in(0,1/2)$ under the normalized tilt. Hence $|u(t,y)|\leq1-2\theta$ and $1-u(t,y)^2\geq4\theta(1-\theta)=:\kappa>0$. Integrating this bound over $[-\sqrt\epsilon,\sqrt\epsilon]$ and using <a href="#eq:terminal-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:terminal-density">[eq:terminal-density]</a> proves <a href="#eq:terminal-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:terminal-layer">[eq:terminal-layer]</a> with $C=2m\kappa$.

It follows that $q(t)-t\leq (1-t)-C\sqrt{1-t}<0$ sufficiently near one. Thus $G(s')<0$ for $s'$ sufficiently near one, contrary to Lemma <a href="#lem:contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contact">13</a>. This also covers $c=0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-no-terminal-plateau-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-no-terminal-plateau</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:full-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-support">1</a>.* Let $S$ be the support in Lemma <a href="#lem:contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contact">13</a>. Since $S$ is relatively closed in $[0,1)$, a nonempty component of its complement is an initial, interior, or terminal interval. On each such interval $\gamma$ is constant. A terminal component is impossible by Lemma <a href="#lem:no-terminal-plateau" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:no-terminal-plateau">14</a>; that lemma also excludes empty support, for which $\gamma=0$.

Consider a remaining gap with endpoints $0\leq a<b<1$ and with $a,b\in S$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contact">13</a>, $q(a)=a$, $q(b)=b$, and $q'(a),q'(b)\leq1$, including the one-sided interpretation at $a=0$. If its constant coefficient is positive, Proposition <a href="#prop:gap-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gap-curvature">11</a> makes $q'$ strictly convex inside the gap. By continuity up to its endpoints, $$q'(t)<\frac{b-t}{b-a}q'(a)+\frac{t-a}{b-a}q'(b)\leq1,
 \qquad a<t<b.$$ Integrating contradicts $q(b)-q(a)=b-a$, as illustrated by the positive area deficit in Figure <a href="#fig:gap-convexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:gap-convexity">1</a>. If the constant coefficient is zero, the strict increase in Proposition <a href="#prop:gap-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gap-curvature">11</a> instead gives $q'(t)<q'(b)\leq1$, with the same contradiction.

It remains only to consider an initial component $[0,b)$ when $0\notin S$ and $b\in S$. Here $\gamma=0$ on the gap. Strict increase gives $q'(t)<q'(b)\leq1$ for $0<t<b$, whereas $q(0)=0$ and $q(b)=b$. Integration is again a contradiction. Thus $S=[0,1)$. The contact identities give $q(t)=t$ for $0<t<1$, and the identity at zero follows from symmetry. Differentiating this identity using Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a> gives $\mathbb E\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(t,X_t)^2=1$, including the right derivative at zero. Finally, every nonempty interval $(a,b)$ with $0\leq a<b<1$ has positive $\mu$ mass, so $\gamma(b)-\gamma(a)=\mu((a,b])>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:density"></a>
#### Smoothness and positivity of the density

The smoothness conclusion and its deduction from saturated consistency appear in Chen (H.-B. Chen 2026, Theorem 1.3 and Proposition 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Chen2026](#ref-Chen2026)]. We include the argument to show how Theorem <a href="#thm:full-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-support">1</a> determines the regularity of the minimizing order parameter. The polynomial-moment differentiation follows Auffinger and Chen (Auffinger and Chen 2015a, Lemma 2 and the proof of Theorem 2(ii)) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[ACProperties2015](#ref-ACProperties2015)], in its zero-temperature form in Chen (H.-B. Chen 2026, Appendix B.6) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Chen2026](#ref-Chen2026)]; the Hessian semimartingale identity is also given by Chen, Handschy, and Lerman (W.-K. Chen, Handschy, and Lerman 2018, Lemma 3) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[CHL2018](#ref-CHL2018)]. We then use the coercive inequality of Section <a href="#cert:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:section">4</a> to prove that the density is strictly positive at every positive time below one.

<a id="stmt-cor-smooth-measure"></a>
<div id="cor:smooth-measure" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:gamma-moment-ratio"></a>
<a id="eq:positive-density-bound"></a>
**Corollary 15** (Smooth positive density of the minimizing measure). *Let $\gamma\in\mathcal U$ minimize $\mathcal P$, and let $X$ be its controlled diffusion. Then $\gamma(0)=0$ and $\gamma$ is smooth on $[0,1)$, with derivatives at zero understood from the right. Moreover, $$\label{eq:gamma-moment-ratio}
 \gamma(t)=
 \frac{\mathbb E[\Phi_{\gamma,xxx}(t,X_t)^2]}
      {2\mathbb E[\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(t,X_t)^3]},
 \qquad 0\leq t<1.$$ Consequently $\mu_\gamma(\mathrm dt)=\gamma'(t)\,\mathrm dt$ on $[0,1)$, where $\gamma'$ is smooth and nonnegative. In fact, for $0<t<1$, $$\label{eq:positive-density-bound}
 2\gamma'(t)\mathbb E[\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(t,X_t)^3]
 \geq \frac{\gamma(t)^2}{1000}
                \mathbb E[\Phi_{\gamma,xx}(t,X_t)^4]>0.$$ In particular $\gamma'(t)>0$ on $(0,1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-smooth-measure"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-smooth-measure</summary>

<a id="eq:spatial-derivative-martingales"></a>
*Proof.* Write $f_k=\partial_x^k\Phi_\gamma$ for $k\geq1$, and evaluate these functions along $X$ unless otherwise indicated. All spatial derivatives are bounded on each compact time strip by Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>. Differentiating the PDE and applying Itô’s formula gives $$\label{eq:spatial-derivative-martingales}
 \mathrm df_k(t,X_t)
 =-\frac{\gamma(t)}2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k}{j}
       f_{j+1}(t,X_t)f_{k-j+1}(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt
       +f_{k+1}(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dB_t.$$ The sum is empty for $k=1$. For general integrable $\gamma$, this identity follows by the finite-step approximation and Itô isometry used in Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a>. On a fixed strip the spatial factors converge uniformly along the coupled paths, the time coefficients converge in $L^1$, and the stochastic integrals converge in $L^2$.

For $a=f_2$ and $b=f_3$, the identity is $\mathrm da=-\gamma a^2\,\mathrm dt+b\,\mathrm dB_t$. Hence $$\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^2
 =\mathbb Eb(t,X_t)^2-2\gamma(t)\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^3
 \quad\hbox{for almost every }t<1.$$ The left side is zero by Theorem <a href="#thm:full-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-support">1</a>. Convexity gives $a\geq0$, and the same theorem gives $\mathbb Ea^2=1$, so $\mathbb Ea^3\geq(\mathbb Ea^2)^{3/2}=1$. The ratio on the right of <a href="#eq:gamma-moment-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gamma-moment-ratio">[eq:gamma-moment-ratio]</a> is therefore well defined and continuous on every compact time strip. Its almost-everywhere equality with $\gamma$ extends to every time by right continuity. In particular $\gamma$ is continuous. At time zero, evenness gives $b(0,0)=0$, so the formula yields $\gamma(0)=0$.

<a id="eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap"></a>
To bootstrap regularity without assuming time derivatives of $\gamma$, consider all polynomial moments of the spatial derivatives at once. For a polynomial $P=P(y_1,\ldots,y_m)$, define $$M_P(t)=\mathbb EP(f_1(t,X_t),\ldots,f_m(t,X_t)).$$ In formal variables $y_1,y_2,\ldots$, set $$\begin{aligned}
 D_k(y)&=-\frac12\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{k}{j}
                           y_{j+1}y_{k-j+1},\\
 Q_P(y)&=\frac12\sum_{k,l=1}^m
              (\partial_k\partial_lP)(y)y_{k+1}y_{l+1},\\
 R_P(y)&=\sum_{k=1}^m(\partial_kP)(y)D_k(y).
 \end{aligned}$$ These are polynomials in finitely many variables, with $D_1=0$. Itô’s product rule and <a href="#eq:spatial-derivative-martingales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-derivative-martingales">[eq:spatial-derivative-martingales]</a> give $$\label{eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap}
 M_P(t)-M_P(0)=\int_0^t
       \bigl(M_{Q_P}(s)+\gamma(s)M_{R_P}(s)\bigr)\,\mathrm ds.$$ Every such moment is finite and continuous on each compact strip. Since $\gamma$ is continuous, <a href="#eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap">[eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap]</a> makes every $M_P$ continuously differentiable; the ratio <a href="#eq:gamma-moment-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gamma-moment-ratio">[eq:gamma-moment-ratio]</a> then makes $\gamma$ continuously differentiable. More generally, if $\gamma$ and all these moments are $C^k$, the integrand in <a href="#eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap">[eq:polynomial-moment-bootstrap]</a> is $C^k$ for every $P$, making all moments $C^{k+1}$. The same ratio makes $\gamma$ $C^{k+1}$. Induction proves smoothness, including right derivatives at zero.

<a id="eq:density-curvature-identity"></a>
Smoothness and $\gamma(0)=0$ identify its Stieltjes measure with $\gamma'(t)\,\mathrm dt$, and monotonicity gives $\gamma'\geq0$. It remains to prove strict positivity away from zero. Set $d=f_4$. The polynomial-moment identities give $$\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\mathbb Eb^2=\mathbb Ed^2-6\gamma\mathbb E(ab^2),
 \qquad
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\mathbb Ea^3=3\mathbb E(ab^2)-3\gamma\mathbb Ea^4.$$ Differentiating $\mathbb Eb^2=2\gamma\mathbb Ea^3$ therefore yields $$\label{eq:density-curvature-identity}
 2\gamma'\mathbb Ea^3
  =\mathbb Ed^2-12\gamma\mathbb E(ab^2)+6\gamma^2\mathbb Ea^4.$$ All factors in this identity are evaluated at $(t,X_t)$.

Fix $t\in(0,1)$ and put $c=\gamma(t)$. Full support implies $c>0$. We next apply the integrated coercivity estimate at this fixed time, although $\gamma$ need not be constant on a neighborhood of $t$. Choose $\delta_n\downarrow0$ with $[t-\delta_n,t+\delta_n]\subset(0,1)$. Replace $\gamma$ on $[t-\delta_n,t+\delta_n)$ by the value $c$. This preserves monotonicity, since $\gamma(s)\leq c$ for $s<t$ and $\gamma(s)\geq c$ for $s>t$, and the change tends to zero in $L^1$. Outside that interval, truncate at a height greater than $c$, impose a positive lower cutoff less than $c$, and approximate separately on the left and right by nondecreasing finite step functions with values at most $c$ and at least $c$, respectively. Let the truncation height tend to infinity, the lower cutoff tend to zero, and the discretization error tend to zero. The resulting positive bounded nondecreasing coefficients $\gamma_n$ converge to $\gamma$ in $L^1$ and equal $c$ on a neighborhood of $t$ for every $n$.

Use terminal data $M_n^{-1}\log(2\cosh(M_nx))$, with $M_n\geq\|\gamma_n\|_\infty$ and $M_n\to\infty$, and let $a_n,b_n,d_n$ and $X^n$ denote the corresponding spatial derivatives and diffusion. At time $t$, the scaled derivatives in Proposition <a href="#cert:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:coercivity">10</a> are $v_n=ca_n$, $w_n=cb_n$, and $z_n=cd_n$. Thus that proposition and Lemmas <a href="#shape:backward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:backward">6</a>–<a href="#shape:ibp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="shape:ibp">8</a> give $$\mathbb E[d_n^2-12c a_n b_n^2+6c^2a_n^4](t,X_t^n)
       \geq\frac{c^2}{1000}\mathbb Ea_n(t,X_t^n)^4.$$ The derivative bounds and convergence on a fixed compact strip from Lemmas <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a> and <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a> do not depend on the widths $\delta_n$ of the artificial plateaus. They therefore allow passage to the limit in every expectation in the last display. Combine the limiting inequality with <a href="#eq:density-curvature-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-curvature-identity">[eq:density-curvature-identity]</a> and $c=\gamma(t)$ to obtain <a href="#eq:positive-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-density-bound">[eq:positive-density-bound]</a>. Its right side is strictly positive because $c>0$ and $\mathbb Ea^4\geq(\mathbb Ea^2)^2=1$. The proof asserts only $\gamma'(0)\geq0$ at the left endpoint. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Martingale representation and optimization consequences

Full support gives the gradient martingale exactly the second-moment profile of Brownian motion: $\mathbb Eu_\gamma(t,X_t)^2=t$. Its correlation with the driving Brownian motion recovers the ground-state value. We first prove this identity and then approximate it using a single $\{-1,1\}$-valued function of finitely many independent Gaussians. The gradient-martingale and normalized Euler approach is developed in Montanari (Montanari 2019, secs. 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Montanari2019](#ref-Montanari2019)] and El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke (El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke 2021, secs. 5–6 of the cited preprint) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[EAMS2021](#ref-EAMS2021)]. Here Theorem <a href="#thm:full-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-support">1</a> supplies the required zero-temperature second-moment normalization.

<a id="stmt-cor-value"></a>
<div id="cor:value" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:value-identities"></a>
**Corollary 16** (Martingale representation of the value). *Let $\gamma$ be a minimizer, let $X$ be its diffusion, and write $u=\Phi_{\gamma,x}$ and $a=\Phi_{\gamma,xx}$. There is a bounded martingale terminal value $U_1$ such that $u(t,X_t)\to U_1$ almost surely and in $L^2$ as $t\uparrow1$, with $U_1^2=1$ almost surely. Moreover, $$\label{eq:value-identities}
 \begin{aligned}
 P_*&=\mathbb E|X_1|-\int_0^1t\gamma(t)\,\mathrm dt\\
    &=\mathbb E[U_1 B_1]
     =\int_0^1\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt.
 \end{aligned}$$ The last integral is finite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-value"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-value</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a> gives the bounded martingale $u(t,X_t)=\int_0^t a(s,X_s)\,\mathrm dB_s$. By Theorem <a href="#thm:full-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-support">1</a>, its second moment is $t$ and $\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^2=1$. Martingale convergence therefore gives $U_1$ with $|U_1|\leq1$ and $\mathbb EU_1^2=1$, hence $U_1^2=1$ almost surely. The diffusion converges to $X_1$ almost surely and in $L^2$, because its drift has deterministic integrable bound $\gamma$.

The uniform terminal bound <a href="#eq:terminal-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:terminal-uniform">[eq:terminal-uniform]</a> and convexity show that $u(t,x_t)\to\operatorname{sgn}(x)$ whenever $x_t\to x\ne0$. Indeed, for a fixed small $h>0$, the derivative of a convex function at $x_t$ lies between its left and right difference quotients of step $h$; uniform convergence to $|\cdot|$ makes both limits equal to the sign when the interval stays away from zero. Consequently $U_1X_1=|X_1|$ almost surely, with no need to exclude $X_1=0$.

Itô’s formula along the optimal diffusion, justified on compact strips as in Lemma <a href="#lem:diffusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diffusion">3</a>, gives $$\mathbb E\Phi_\gamma(t,X_t)
  =\Phi_\gamma(0,0)+\frac12\int_0^t\gamma(s)
                                  \mathbb Eu(s,X_s)^2\,\mathrm ds.$$ Let $t\uparrow1$. The terminal bound and $L^1$ convergence of $X_t$ identify the left side with $\mathbb E|X_1|$. Substitute $\mathbb Eu(s,X_s)^2=s$ and the definition of $\mathcal P$ to obtain the first line of <a href="#eq:value-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:value-identities">[eq:value-identities]</a>. Since $X_1=B_1+\int_0^1\gamma(s)u(s,X_s)\,\mathrm ds$, martingality and Fubini’s theorem yield $$\mathbb E[U_1X_1]=\mathbb E[U_1B_1]
       +\int_0^1\gamma(s)\mathbb E[u(s,X_s)^2]\,\mathrm ds.$$ All terms are integrable because $|u|,|U_1|\leq1$ and $\gamma\in L^1$. Finally the $L^2$ stochastic-integral identity at time one implies $\mathbb E[U_1B_1]=\int_0^1\mathbb Ea(s,X_s)\,\mathrm ds$. The integral is absolutely bounded by one using $\mathbb E|a(s,X_s)|\leq(\mathbb Ea(s,X_s)^2)^{1/2}=1$. ◻

</details>

The last expression in <a href="#eq:value-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:value-identities">[eq:value-identities]</a> is the expected covariation of the gradient martingale with its driving Brownian motion. To approximate it, replace the diffusion by an Euler scheme on a fixed strip $[0,T]$, where $T<1$. The continuum identity $\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^2=1$ makes each martingale increment have variance equal to its time step. The Euler scheme has only an approximate version of this normalization, so we normalize its Gaussian-weighted directions to unit variance.

One additional independent Gaussian then rounds the Euler terminal gradient to a spin while preserving its conditional mean. The coefficients $A_j$ below measure this spin’s correlations with the normalized martingale directions; the coefficients $B_i$ measure its correlations with the original Gaussian coordinates. Their adjacent-index product converges to the same covariation integral. These are scalar Gaussian coefficients, not an implementation on the finite-spin disorder.

On this fixed strip, $u,a$ and all their spatial derivatives are bounded; the Gaussian increments remain unbounded. We first refine the mesh and only afterwards let $T$ approach one. The mesh count $N$ is distinct from the spin-system size $n$ in the introduction.

<a id="stmt-prop-gaussian"></a>
<div id="prop:gaussian" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:euler"></a>
<a id="eq:finite-value"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Finite Gaussian coefficients). *Fix $0<T<1$, a minimizer $\gamma$, and $u,a$ as above. For an integer $N\geq1$ put $\Delta=T/N$ and $t_j=j\Delta$. On independent standard normal variables $Z_1,\ldots,Z_{N+1}$, define <a id="eq:normalized-increment"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 Y_0&=0,\qquad
 Y_j=Y_{j-1}+\sqrt\Delta Z_j
      +\Delta\gamma(t_{j-1})u(t_{j-1},Y_{j-1}),
       &&1\leq j\leq N,\label{eq:euler}\\
 c_j&=\bigl(\mathbb Ea(t_{j-1},Y_{j-1})^2\bigr)^{-1/2}>0,
 \qquad g_j=c_j a(t_{j-1},Y_{j-1})Z_j.
       \label{eq:normalized-increment}
\end{aligned}$$ For all sufficiently large $N$ these constants are finite and positive. For these mesh counts, let $F_{\rm G}$ denote the standard normal distribution function and set $$\begin{gathered}
 F_N=\operatorname{sgn}\bigl(u(T,Y_N)+2F_{\rm G}(Z_{N+1})-1\bigr),\\
 A_j=\mathbb E[F_Ng_j],\qquad B_i=\mathbb E[F_NZ_i],\qquad A_0=0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Here $1\leq j\leq N$ and $1\leq i\leq N+1$ in the definitions of $A_j$ and $B_i$. The value of the sign at zero is immaterial. Then $$\label{eq:finite-value}
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{j=0}^N A_jB_{j+1}
       =\int_0^T\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)\,\mathrm dt.$$ Letting $T\uparrow1$ on the right gives $P_*$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-gaussian"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-gaussian</summary>

<a id="eq:euler-convergence"></a>
*Proof.* *Euler convergence and normalization.* Use Brownian increments $\sqrt\Delta Z_j=B_{t_j}-B_{t_{j-1}}$ to couple <a href="#eq:euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler">[eq:euler]</a> to $X$ up to time $T$. Lemma <a href="#lem:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regularity">2</a>, on a slightly larger strip, bounds all spatial derivatives of $u,a$. Their almost-everywhere time derivatives given by the PDE are bounded there, so $u,a$ are uniformly Lipschitz in time and space. The drift $\gamma u$ is spatially Lipschitz with a common bound. For its time dependence, it is enough to use that $\gamma$ is bounded and monotone. If $\pi_N(s)=t_{j-1}$ on $[t_{j-1},t_j)$, then $$\int_0^T|\gamma(s)-\gamma(\pi_N(s))|\,\mathrm ds
       \leq\Delta\bigl(\gamma(T)-\gamma(0)\bigr).$$ Also $\mathbb E|X_s-X_{\pi_N(s)}|^2\leq C\Delta$. Subtract the two integral drift equations at the grid points, use these bounds and the Lipschitz bounds, and apply discrete Gronwall. This gives $$\label{eq:euler-convergence}
 \mathbb E\max_{j\leq N}|Y_j-X_{t_j}|^2\longrightarrow0.$$ More explicitly, the $L^2$ norm of the sum of local drift errors is at most $C\sqrt\Delta+C\Delta$; the remaining errors satisfy $e_j\leq C\Delta\sum_{i<j}e_i+o(1)$, which proves the claim.

Uniform convergence in <a href="#eq:euler-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-convergence">[eq:euler-convergence]</a>, boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of $a$, and $\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)^2=1$ imply $\max_{j\leq N}|c_j-1|\to0$. The $g_j$ form an orthonormal family in $L^2$: they have unit second moment by definition and zero conditional mean given the previous increments. Itô isometry, <a href="#eq:euler-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-convergence">[eq:euler-convergence]</a>, and the same continuity estimates give $$M_N:=\sum_{j=1}^N\sqrt\Delta\,a(t_{j-1},Y_{j-1})Z_j
      =u(T,Y_N)+o_{L^2}(1).$$ *Rounding and the two coefficient vectors.* The independent random variable $F_{\rm G}(Z_{N+1})$ is uniform on $(0,1)$. For any $v\in[-1,1]$, the sign of $v+2U-1$, with $U$ uniform on $(0,1)$, has mean $v$. Hence $\mathbb E[F_N\mid Z_1,\ldots,Z_N]=u(T,Y_N)$ exactly. This preserves every correlation with a function of the first $N$ Gaussians. Bessel’s inequality applied to $u(T,Y_N)-M_N$ against the two orthonormal families $(g_j)_{j=1}^N$ and $(Z_i)_{i=1}^N$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 (A_j)_{j=1}^N&=(\sqrt\Delta/c_j)_{j=1}^N+o_{\ell^2}(1),\\
 (B_i)_{i=1}^N&=(\sqrt\Delta\,\mathbb Ea(t_{i-1},Y_{i-1}))_{i=1}^N
                                      +o_{\ell^2}(1).
