\end{aligned}$$ For the second equality, terms of $M_N$ before $i$ are independent of $Z_i$, and those after $i$ have zero conditional mean; the $i$th term contributes the stated expectation. The first equality follows from orthogonality of the $g_j$ and $M_N=\sum_j\sqrt\Delta\,g_j/c_j$.

*The shifted product.* The first coefficient vector is asymptotically the constant vector with entries $\sqrt\Delta$, because $c_j\to1$ uniformly. The second has entries $\sqrt\Delta$ times the expected Hessian at the preceding mesh point. Pairing $A_j$ with $B_{j+1}$ therefore selects the expected Hessian at $t_j$, which is a Riemann-sum value for the target integral. To justify the accumulated errors, the vectors in both displays have bounded $\ell^2$ norm. Cauchy–Schwarz shows that their errors contribute $o(1)$ to the shifted scalar product with $1\leq j<N$. The first display also gives $A_N=\sqrt\Delta/c_N+o(1)=o(1)$. The remaining term therefore obeys $|A_NB_{N+1}|\leq|A_N|=o(1)$, since $|B_{N+1}|\leq1$. Thus the sum in <a href="#eq:finite-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-value">[eq:finite-value]</a> differs by $o(1)$ from $$\Delta\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{\mathbb Ea(t_j,Y_j)}{c_j}.$$ This converges to the stated integral by <a href="#eq:euler-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler-convergence">[eq:euler-convergence]</a>, uniform convergence of $c_j$ to one, and continuity of $t\mapsto\mathbb Ea(t,X_t)$. Corollary <a href="#cor:value" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:value">16</a> gives its limit as $T\uparrow1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:algorithm-application"></a>
#### An application of the existing optimization theorem

The full-support theorem also verifies the hypothesis of an existing algorithmic result. We use the operation count of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke (El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke 2021, Remark 2.1, Theorem 3, and Corollary 2.2 of the cited preprint) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[EAMS2021](#ref-EAMS2021)]: real sums and products have unit cost, and the scalar functions and normalization constants are those prescribed in their algorithm for the chosen accuracy. This is an instance-size bound; the argument below supplies no separate complexity estimate for numerical preprocessing of those functions or for finite-precision arithmetic.

<a id="stmt-cor-algorithm"></a>
<div id="cor:algorithm" class="corollary">

**Corollary 18** (Asymptotically optimal SK optimization). *For every fixed $\epsilon>0$, there is a randomized algorithm with operation count at most $C(\epsilon)n^2$ in the model just described, where $C(\epsilon)$ is independent of $n$, that takes the Gaussian couplings of $H_n$ as input and returns $\sigma^{\rm alg}\in\{-1,1\}^n$ such that $$\Pr\left\{
 H_n(\sigma^{\rm alg})\geq
 \max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^n}H_n(\sigma)-\epsilon n
 \right\}\longrightarrow1
 \qquad(n\to\infty).$$ The probability includes the couplings and the algorithm’s randomness.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-algorithm"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-algorithm</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#thm:full-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-support">1</a> gives a strictly increasing minimizer in $\mathcal U$, exactly the no-overlap-gap hypothesis in (El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke 2021, Assumption 2) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[EAMS2021](#ref-EAMS2021)] for $\xi(t)=t^2/2$. We apply their Corollary 2.2, with its operation count from Theorem 3.

To match their Gaussian quadratic input, form a symmetric matrix $A$ with $A_{ij}=J_{ij}/\sqrt n$ for $i<j$ and independent diagonal entries $A_{ii}=\sqrt{2/n}\,G_i$, where the $G_i$ are independent standard Gaussians. The quadratic extension $\widehat H_n(x)=\frac12x^{\mathsf T}Ax$ has covariance $(2n)^{-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf T}\tau)^2$ on the hypercube, and $$\widehat H_n(\sigma)
   =H_n(\sigma)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}\sum_{i=1}^nG_i.$$ The second term is independent of $\sigma$, so every optimization gap is identical for $H_n$ and $\widehat H_n$.

Evaluating $\nabla\widehat H_n(x)=Ax$ costs $O(n^2)$ operations. The part with distinct indices is $H_n(x)$, and evaluating one coordinate of its gradient costs $O(n)$. Thus the bound $C(\epsilon)(\chi+n)+n\chi_1$ in the cited theorem is $C(\epsilon)n^2$, after changing the constant. Its probability guarantee and the equality of the optimization gaps give the assertion. ◻

</details>

This corollary invokes the finite-disorder message-passing theorem of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke. Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian">17</a> supplies the separate scalar approximation proved above.

    {
      "caption": "The contradiction on a hypothetical support gap with $γ = c > 0$. Strict convexity puts $q′$ below its endpoint chord, which is at most $1$. The shaded deficit is positive, whereas contact at both endpoints requires $∫_{a}^{b}q′(t) dt = q(b) − q(a) = b − a$. The endpoint derivatives need not equal $1$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:gap-convexity",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:gap-convexity",
          "exact_quote": "The contradiction on a hypothetical support gap with\n$\\gamma=c>0$. Strict convexity puts $q'$ below its endpoint chord,\nwhich is at most $1$. The shaded deficit is positive, whereas contact\nat both endpoints requires $\\int_a^bq'(t)\\,\\dd t=q",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,line cap=round]\n  \\fill[blue!8] (0,3.1) -- (8,3.1) -- (8,2.2)\n     .. controls (5.3333,.25) and (2.6667,.10) .. (0,1.8) -- cycle;\n  \\draw[->] (-1.9,0) -- (8.7,0) node[right] {$t$};\n  \\draw[->] (-1.9,0) -- (-1.9,3.5) node[above] {$q'(t)$};\n  \\draw[gray!70] (-1.9,3.1) -- (8.25,3.1);\n  \\node[left] at (-1.9,3.1) {$1$};\n  \\draw[densely dashed,gray!75] (0,1.8) -- (8,2.2)\n    node[midway,above=3pt,text=black,fill=blue!8,inner sep=1pt]\n       {endpoint chord};\n  \\draw[very thick,blue!60!black] (0,1.8)\n    .. controls (2.6667,.10) and (5.3333,.25) .. (8,2.2);\n  \\draw[dotted,gray!80] (0,0) -- (0,1.8);\n  \\draw[dotted,gray!80] (8,0) -- (8,2.2);\n  \\fill (0,1.8) circle (1.6pt);\n  \\fill (8,2.2) circle (1.6pt);\n  \\node[below] at (0,0) {$a$};\n  \\node[below] at (8,0) {$b$};\n  \\node[above left=3pt] at (0,1.8) {$q'(a)\\le1$};\n  \\node[above right=3pt] at (8,2.2) {$q'(b)\\le1$};\n  \\node[below,blue!60!black] at (4,.58) {strictly convex $q'$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D Cartesian plot with a horizontal axis labeled $t$ and a vertical axis labeled $q'(t)$. On the vertical axis, a tick is labeled $1$, with a horizontal dotted line extending across above the interval $[a,b]$. On the horizontal axis, two points $a$ and $b$ are marked. Above $a$, a vertical dashed line reaches $(a, q'(a))$, labeled $q'(a) \\le 1$. Above $b$, a vertical dashed line reaches $(b, q'(b))$, labeled $q'(b) \\le 1$. A straight dashed line segment connects $(a, q'(a))$ and $(b, q'(b))$, labeled \"endpoint chord\". Below this chord, a solid blue curve connecting $(a, q'(a))$ and $(b, q'(b))$ sags downward, labeled \"strictly convex $q'$\". The region between the dashed endpoint chord and the solid blue curve is filled with light purple/blue shading."

      "source_pm_id": "PM-281.2",
      "source_quote": "At positive temperature, Zhou (Zhou 2026, Theorem 1) [[Zhou2026](#ref-Zhou2026)] established an interval-support result near the critical temperature, and Lopatto (Lopatto 2026, Theorem 1.1) [[Lopatto2026](#ref-Lopatto2026)] established full interval support throughout the zero-field SK low-temperature phase.",
